I’m not sure. I mean… I think she’s kind of the backdrop to everything right now, so it’s hard to separate it out.

That’s when this all seemed to start.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “most days” is hard for me to measure. Since she left, things have felt off, definitely. The house is quiet in this strange way—like even the refrigerator sounds louder, which sounds ridiculous.\n\nI keep finding myself noticing little things she left behind, like a hair tie or one of those water bottles she never washed properly, and then I end up thinking about whether I was enough for her, whether I did it right. So… yeah, I’ve felt low, I guess. But I don’t know how to count the days exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I think she’s kind of the backdrop to everything right now, so it’s hard to separate it out.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally ordinary—standing in the kitchen, looking at the mail, and then I can’t tell if I’m sad because of her leaving or just because everything feels… heavy. And then I get distracted thinking about how I used to sort the mail with my wife years ago, we had this little basket that was always overflowing, and I hated that thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t really know how to tell when I’m not thinking about her, because it all sort of blends together.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you wake up in the morning, do you feel depressed before you start thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not really sure. Mornings are… fuzzy. I wake up and there’s this moment where I’m just aware the room is quiet, and then my brain starts filling in the reasons, you know?

Sometimes I’m thinking about her right away, sometimes I’m thinking about whether I left the coffee filter in from yesterday, or how the light comes through the blinds differently this time of year. My wife used to hate those blinds, actually—we talked about replacing them for years and never did.

So… I don’t know if the depressed feeling comes first or the thoughts about my daughter come first. It’s hard for me to separate them that cleanly.

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I still do little things here and there, but the normal stuff is… stacking up.

Bills are sitting in piles, and I keep telling myself I’ll deal with them after coffee, or after lunch, or tomorrow. There are house projects I started and then just sort of abandoned. I used to be pretty good about keeping track of all that, actually—had folders and lists and everything. My daughter used to tease me about the labels on the folders.

And I’m not really seeing people either. There were a few people from book club, and I used to go on rides sometimes, but I’ve just… stopped. I don’t answer messages the way I should.

I guess the part that makes me uncomfortable saying it out loud is that I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. So no, I don’t think I’m keeping up, and I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, my daughter leaving is the thing my mind grabs onto, so it’s hard to pretend that isn’t part of it, but even when I’m not actively thinking about her, there’s this… verdict in my head, I guess.

Yeah… yes. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts of it.

I used to really look forward to working on the model railway. I had these little sections laid out on the table, tiny buildings, track pieces, all of that. There’s one part where I was trying to make the tunnel look realistic, and I kept buying the wrong kind of glue for it—there are more kinds of glue than anyone needs, honestly. But now it just sits there half-built. I walk past it and think I should do something, and then… nothing.

And Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least argue about them together. Now even turning a game on feels pointless. Like I’ll pick up the remote and then set it back down because I can’t imagine caring what happens.

It’s been about a month now, I think.\n\nIt started after my daughter left for college out of state. Since then it hasn’t really let up. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone does. There were times I was down for a while, especially around work changes and family things, but I don’t know if I’d call it like this.\n\nTwo weeks or longer… I’m trying to think. It’s funny, I can remember where I put some ridiculous receipt from years ago, but not how long I felt bad in, say, 2016. I’d have to really think about it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly the way this past month has.\n\nDoctor: During the past month, have you felt depressed for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “most of the day,” exactly. I wake up and there’s this moment where I’m just aware the room is quiet, and then my brain starts filling in the reasons, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m thinking about her right away, sometimes I’m thinking about whether I left the coffee filter in from yesterday, or how the light comes through the blinds differently this time of year. My wife used to hate those blinds, actually—we talked about replacing them for years and never did.\n\nSo… I don’t know if the depressed feeling comes first or the thoughts about my daughter come first. It’s hard for me to separate them that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to keep up with your usual daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do little things here and there, but the normal stuff is… stacking up.\n\nBills are sitting in piles, and I keep telling myself I’ll deal with them after coffee, or after lunch, or tomorrow. There are house projects I started and then just sort of abandoned. I used to be pretty good about keeping track of all that, actually—had folders and lists and everything. My daughter used to tease me about the labels on the folders.\n\nAnd I’m not really seeing people either. There were a few people from book club, and I used to go on rides sometimes, but I’ve just… stopped. I don’t answer messages the way I should.\n\nI guess the part that makes me uncomfortable saying it out loud is that I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. So no, I don’t think I’m keeping up, and I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel like you’ve failed and don’t deserve much of a future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my daughter leaving is the thing my mind grabs onto, so it’s hard to pretend that isn’t part of it, but even when I’m not actively thinking about her, there’s this… verdict in my head, I guess.\n\nLike I failed as a father. And not just that—I failed at being someone useful, someone who has a reason to be here. I came in because I can’t shake that. It loops and loops until it feels like I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about all the practical things, like the boxes in the garage I never sorted, or old tax folders—sorry, I know that’s off track. But yes. The feeling is there even when I’m not specifically thinking about her.\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts of it.\n\nI used to really look forward to working on the model railway. I had these little sections laid out on the table, tiny buildings, track pieces, all of that. There’s one part where I was trying to make the tunnel look realistic, and I kept buying the wrong kind of glue for it—there are more kinds of glue than anyone needs, honestly. But now it just sits there half-built. I walk past it and think I should do something, and then… nothing.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least argue about them together. Now even turning a game on feels pointless. Like I’ll pick up the remote and then set it back down because I can’t imagine caring what happens.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day. It’s been about a month now, I think.\n\nIt started after my daughter left for college out of state. I remember the day pretty clearly because I kept fussing with the boxes in the car, like that was going to make any difference, and then afterward the house just felt… too quiet. Since then it hasn’t really let up. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone does. There were times I was down for a while, especially around work changes and family things, but I don’t know if I’d call it like this.\n\nTwo weeks or longer… I’m trying to think. It’s funny, I can remember where I put some ridiculous receipt from years ago, but not how long I felt bad in, say, 2016. I’d have to really think about it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly the way this past month has.\n\nDoctor: During the past month, have you felt depressed for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “most of the day,” exactly. It’s there a lot, that heavy feeling, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like realizing the porch light has been out for weeks or trying to remember whether I paid the water bill, and I’ll think, does that count as not feeling depressed for a minute?\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer. A lot of days have felt pretty low, but “most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not completely sure how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would normally expect after a child leaves for college?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, people say it’s normal to feel sad when your kid leaves, right? My sister kept saying, “Empty nest, it’ll pass,” and then she started talking about repainting her son’s old room, which… I don’t know, I can’t even imagine doing that.\n\nBut stronger than normal? I don’t know what normal is supposed to feel like. I know it hit me hard, harder than I expected, but whether that’s “too much” or just me not handling it well… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When you’re occupied with something else and not thinking about your daughter leaving, does the depression still feel just as strong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. Like, if I’m doing something else, sometimes I’ll realize I’ve gone ten minutes thinking about the grocery list or whether the car needs servicing — actually I meant to call about that last week — and then it sort of comes back into focus again.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s just as strong the whole time or if I’m just not noticing it for a bit. It doesn’t feel like I get a clean break from it, but I’m not sure how to rate it when I’m distracted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. There were times when I felt pretty down, like when things were changing or I wasn’t sure what I was doing with myself, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks or longer… I honestly don’t know how to pin that down.

I’m bad with timelines. I’ll think something lasted a week and then realize it was a month, or the other way around. Like, I still keep old calendars in a drawer for no reason, and sometimes I look at them and think, what was I even doing that year?

So… maybe? But I can’t say clearly, no. I don’t have a specific time I can point to and say, yes, that was two weeks straight.

So… yeah, I’ve felt low, I guess. But I don’t know how to count the days exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I think she’s kind of the backdrop to everything right now, so it’s hard to separate it out.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally ordinary—standing in the kitchen, looking at the mail, and then I can’t tell if I’m sad because of her leaving or just because everything feels… heavy. And then I get distracted thinking about how I used to sort the mail with my wife years ago, we had this little basket that was always overflowing, and I hated that thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t really know how to tell when I’m not thinking about her, because it all sort of blends together.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you feel depressed before you start thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. My wife used to hate those blinds, actually—we talked about replacing them for years and never did.\n\nSo… I don’t know if the depressed feeling comes first or the thoughts about my daughter come first. It’s hard for me to separate them that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to keep up with your usual daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My daughter used to tease me about the labels on the folders.\n\nAnd I’m not really seeing people either. There were a few people from book club, and I used to go on rides sometimes, but I’ve just… stopped. I don’t answer messages the way I should.\n\nI guess the part that makes me uncomfortable saying it out loud is that I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. So no, I don’t think I’m keeping up, and I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel like you’ve failed and don’t deserve much of a future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my daughter leaving is the thing my mind grabs onto, so it’s hard to pretend that isn’t part of it, but even when I’m not actively thinking about her, there’s this… verdict in my head, I guess.\n\nLike I failed as a father. And not just that—I failed at being someone useful, someone who has a reason to be here. I came in because I can’t shake that. It loops and loops until it feels like I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about all the practical things, like the boxes in the garage I never sorted, or old tax folders—sorry, I know that’s off track. But yes. The feeling is there even when I’m not specifically thinking about her.\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts of it.\n\nI used to really look forward to working on the model railway. I had these little sections laid out on the table, tiny buildings, track pieces, all of that. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would normally expect after a child leaves for college?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, people say it’s normal to feel sad when your kid leaves, right? My sister kept saying, “Empty nest, it’ll pass,” and then she started talking about repainting her son’s old room, which… I don’t know, I can’t even imagine doing that.\n\nBut stronger than normal? I don’t know what normal is supposed to feel like. I know it hit me hard, harder than I expected, but whether that’s “too much” or just me not handling it well… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When you’re occupied with something else and not thinking about your daughter leaving, does the depression still feel just as strong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. Like, if I’m doing something else, sometimes I’ll realize I’ve gone ten minutes thinking about the grocery list or whether the car needs servicing — actually I meant to call about that last week — and then it sort of comes back into focus again.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s just as strong the whole time or if I’m just not noticing it for a bit. It doesn’t feel like I get a clean break from it, but I’m not sure how to rate it when I’m distracted.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, sure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. “Out of proportion” is hard for me to judge, because she’s my daughter, you know? It feels enormous to me, but then I start thinking maybe any parent would feel that way if the house suddenly got quiet.

And then I think about how other people handle it—my neighbor was joking about turning his son’s room into an office, and I remember thinking, how are you joking about that? But he also has this whole workshop in his garage, he’s always building birdhouses or shelves or whatever, so maybe he just has more to keep him busy.

So… I can’t really say if it’s out of proportion. It feels like too much, but I don’t know compared to what.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I just can’t get away from this feeling that I’ve failed. As a father, mainly, and as a useful person. It keeps going around in my head, over and over.\n\nI used to think if you worked hard enough, you know, you’d have something to point to, something that proved you mattered. My dad used to say that—well, not exactly that, but he was always fixing things, couldn’t sit still. Anyway… I don’t feel like that. I feel like I don’t deserve much of a future, if I’m honest.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. Not like—well, I don’t know how to say it without making it sound dramatic. I’ve had these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then the other day I caught myself going through my finances, almost like I was trying to get things in order for someone else, and that… that scared me. I used to be very organized with money anyway, my father was like that too, kept every receipt in little folders, but this felt different. Like I wasn’t just budgeting. It really frightened me.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It really got bad about a month ago, when my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI mean, I knew it was coming, obviously. We bought the dorm stuff and argued about whether she needed this ridiculous little lamp—she did, apparently—and I drove around for weeks acting like it was all normal. But after she left, the house just felt… different. Empty in a way I wasn’t ready for. That’s when this all seemed to start.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “most days” is hard for me to measure. Since she left, things have felt off, definitely. The house is quiet in this strange way—like even the refrigerator sounds louder, which sounds ridiculous.\n\nI keep finding myself noticing little things she left behind, like a hair tie or one of those water bottles she never washed properly, and then I end up thinking about whether I was enough for her, whether I did it right. So… yeah, I’ve felt low, I guess. But I don’t know how to count the days exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I think she’s kind of the backdrop to everything right now, so it’s hard to separate it out.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally ordinary—standing in the kitchen, looking at the mail, and then I can’t tell if I’m sad because of her leaving or just because everything feels… heavy. And then I get distracted thinking about how I used to sort the mail with my wife years ago, we had this little basket that was always overflowing, and I hated that thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t really know how to tell when I’m not thinking about her, because it all sort of blends together.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you feel depressed before you start thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Mornings are… fuzzy. I wake up and there’s this moment where I’m just aware the room is quiet, and then my brain starts filling in the reasons, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m thinking about her right away, sometimes I’m thinking about whether I left the coffee filter in from yesterday, or how the light comes through the blinds differently this time of year. My wife used to hate those blinds, actually—we talked about replacing them for years and never did.\n\nSo… I don’t know if the depressed feeling comes first or the thoughts about my daughter come first. It’s hard for me to separate them that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to keep up with your usual daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do little things here and there, but the normal stuff is… stacking up.\n\nBills are sitting in piles, and I keep telling myself I’ll deal with them after coffee, or after lunch, or tomorrow. There are house projects I started and then just sort of abandoned. I used to be pretty good about keeping track of all that, actually—had folders and lists and everything. My daughter used to tease me about the labels on the folders.\n\nAnd I’m not really seeing people either. There were a few people from book club, and I used to go on rides sometimes, but I’ve just… stopped. I don’t answer messages the way I should.\n\nI guess the part that makes me uncomfortable saying it out loud is that I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. So no, I don’t think I’m keeping up, and I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel like you’ve failed and don’t deserve much of a future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my daughter leaving is the thing my mind grabs onto, so it’s hard to pretend that isn’t part of it, but even when I’m not actively thinking about her, there’s this… verdict in my head, I guess.\n\nLike I failed as a father. And not just that—I failed at being someone useful, someone who has a reason to be here. I came in because I can’t shake that. It loops and loops until it feels like I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about all the practical things, like the boxes in the garage I never sorted, or old tax folders—sorry, I know that’s off track. But yes. The feeling is there even when I’m not specifically thinking about her.\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts of it.\n\nI used to really look forward to working on the model railway. I had these little sections laid out on the table, tiny buildings, track pieces, all of that. There’s one part where I was trying to make the tunnel look realistic, and I kept buying the wrong kind of glue for it—there are more kinds of glue than anyone needs, honestly. But now it just sits there half-built. I walk past it and think I should do something, and then… nothing.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least argue about them together. Now even turning a game on feels pointless. Like I’ll pick up the remote and then set it back down because I can’t imagine caring what happens.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day. It’s been about a month now, I think.\n\nIt started after my daughter left for college out of state. I remember the day pretty clearly because I kept fussing with the boxes in the car, like that was going to make any difference, and then afterward the house just felt… too quiet. Since then it hasn’t really let up. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone does. There were times I was down for a while, especially around work changes and family things, but I don’t know if I’d call it like this.\n\nTwo weeks or longer… I’m trying to think. It’s funny, I can remember where I put some ridiculous receipt from years ago, but not how long I felt bad in, say, 2016. I’d have to really think about it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly the way this past month has.\n\nDoctor: During the past month, have you felt depressed for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “most of the day,” exactly. It’s there a lot, that heavy feeling, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like realizing the porch light has been out for weeks or trying to remember whether I paid the water bill, and I’ll think, does that count as not feeling depressed for a minute?\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer. A lot of days have felt pretty low, but “most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not completely sure how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would normally expect after a child leaves for college?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. So… yeah, I’ve felt low, I guess. But I don’t know how to count the days exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I think she’s kind of the backdrop to everything right now, so it’s hard to separate it out.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally ordinary—standing in the kitchen, looking at the mail, and then I can’t tell if I’m sad because of her leaving or just because everything feels… heavy. And then I get distracted thinking about how I used to sort the mail with my wife years ago, we had this little basket that was always overflowing, and I hated that thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t really know how to tell when I’m not thinking about her, because it all sort of blends together.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you feel depressed before you start thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Mornings are… fuzzy. I wake up and there’s this moment where I’m just aware the room is quiet, and then my brain starts filling in the reasons, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m thinking about her right away, sometimes I’m thinking about whether I left the coffee filter in from yesterday, or how the light comes through the blinds differently this time of year. My wife used to hate those blinds, actually—we talked about replacing them for years and never did.\n\nSo… I don’t know if the depressed feeling comes first or the thoughts about my daughter come first. It’s hard for me to separate them that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to keep up with your usual daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do little things here and there, but the normal stuff is… stacking up.\n\nBills are sitting in piles, and I keep telling myself I’ll deal with them after coffee, or after lunch, or tomorrow. There are house projects I started and then just sort of abandoned. I used to be pretty good about keeping track of all that, actually—had folders and lists and everything. My daughter used to tease me about the labels on the folders.\n\nAnd I’m not really seeing people either. There were a few people from book club, and I used to go on rides sometimes, but I’ve just… stopped. I don’t answer messages the way I should.\n\nI guess the part that makes me uncomfortable saying it out loud is that I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. So no, I don’t think I’m keeping up, and I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel like you’ve failed and don’t deserve much of a future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my daughter leaving is the thing my mind grabs onto, so it’s hard to pretend that isn’t part of it, but even when I’m not actively thinking about her, there’s this… verdict in my head, I guess.\n\nLike I failed as a father. And not just that—I failed at being someone useful, someone who has a reason to be here. I came in because I can’t shake that. It loops and loops until it feels like I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about all the practical things, like the boxes in the garage I never sorted, or old tax folders—sorry, I know that’s off track. But yes. The feeling is there even when I’m not specifically thinking about her.\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts of it.\n\nI used to really look forward to working on the model railway. I had these little sections laid out on the table, tiny buildings, track pieces, all of that. There’s one part where I was trying to make the tunnel look realistic, and I kept buying the wrong kind of glue for it—there are more kinds of glue than anyone needs, honestly. But now it just sits there half-built. I walk past it and think I should do something, and then… nothing.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least argue about them together. Now even turning a game on feels pointless. Like I’ll pick up the remote and then set it back down because I can’t imagine caring what happens.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day. It’s been about a month now, I think.\n\nIt started after my daughter left for college out of state. I remember the day pretty clearly because I kept fussing with the boxes in the car, like that was going to make any difference, and then afterward the house just felt… too quiet. Since then it hasn’t really let up. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone does. There were times I was down for a while, especially around work changes and family things, but I don’t know if I’d call it like this.\n\nTwo weeks or longer… I’m trying to think. It’s funny, I can remember where I put some ridiculous receipt from years ago, but not how long I felt bad in, say, 2016. I’d have to really think about it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly the way this past month has.\n\nDoctor: During the past month, have you felt depressed for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “most of the day,” exactly. It’s there a lot, that heavy feeling, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like realizing the porch light has been out for weeks or trying to remember whether I paid the water bill, and I’ll think, does that count as not feeling depressed for a minute?\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer. A lot of days have felt pretty low, but “most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not completely sure how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would normally expect after a child leaves for college?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, people say it’s normal to feel sad when your kid leaves, right? My sister kept saying, “Empty nest, it’ll pass,” and then she started talking about repainting her son’s old room, which… I don’t know, I can’t even imagine doing that.\n\nBut stronger than normal? I don’t know what normal is supposed to feel like. I know it hit me hard, harder than I expected, but whether that’s “too much” or just me not handling it well… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When you’re occupied with something else and not thinking about your daughter leaving, does the depression still feel just as strong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. Like, if I’m doing something else, sometimes I’ll realize I’ve gone ten minutes thinking about the grocery list or whether the car needs servicing — actually I meant to call about that last week — and then it sort of comes back into focus again.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s just as strong the whole time or if I’m just not noticing it for a bit. It doesn’t feel like I get a clean break from it, but I’m not sure how to rate it when I’m distracted.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, sure. I’ll think something lasted a week and then realize it was a month, or the other way around. I don’t have a specific time I can point to and say, yes, that was two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your reaction has been out of proportion to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Out of proportion” is hard for me to judge, because she’s my daughter, you know? It feels enormous to me, but then I start thinking maybe any parent would feel that way if the house suddenly got quiet.\n\nAnd then I think about how other people handle it—my neighbor was joking about turning his son’s room into an office, and I remember thinking, how are you joking about that? But he also has this whole workshop in his garage, he’s always building birdhouses or shelves or whatever, so maybe he just has more to keep him busy.\n\nSo… I can’t really say if it’s out of proportion. Yeah… definitely. I’m not sleeping like I used to.

That’s actually been one of the stranger parts of it.\n\nI used to really look forward to working on the model railway. I had these little sections laid out on the table, tiny buildings, track pieces, all of that. There’s one part where I was trying to make the tunnel look realistic, and I kept buying the wrong kind of glue for it—there are more kinds of glue than anyone needs, honestly. But now it just sits there half-built. I walk past it and think I should do something, and then… nothing.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least argue about them together. Now even turning a game on feels pointless. Like I’ll pick up the remote and then set it back down because I can’t imagine caring what happens.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day. It’s been about a month now, I think.\n\nIt started after my daughter left for college out of state. I remember the day pretty clearly because I kept fussing with the boxes in the car, like that was going to make any difference, and then afterward the house just felt… too quiet. Since then it hasn’t really let up. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone does. There were times I was down for a while, especially around work changes and family things, but I don’t know if I’d call it like this.\n\nTwo weeks or longer… I’m trying to think. It’s funny, I can remember where I put some ridiculous receipt from years ago, but not how long I felt bad in, say, 2016. I’d have to really think about it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly the way this past month has.\n\nDoctor: During the past month, have you felt depressed for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “most of the day,” exactly. It’s there a lot, that heavy feeling, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like realizing the porch light has been out for weeks or trying to remember whether I paid the water bill, and I’ll think, does that count as not feeling depressed for a minute?\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer. A lot of days have felt pretty low, but “most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not completely sure how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would normally expect after a child leaves for college?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, people say it’s normal to feel sad when your kid leaves, right? My sister kept saying, “Empty nest, it’ll pass,” and then she started talking about repainting her son’s old room, which… I don’t know, I can’t even imagine doing that.\n\nBut stronger than normal? I don’t know what normal is supposed to feel like. I know it hit me hard, harder than I expected, but whether that’s “too much” or just me not handling it well… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When you’re occupied with something else and not thinking about your daughter leaving, does the depression still feel just as strong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. Like, if I’m doing something else, sometimes I’ll realize I’ve gone ten minutes thinking about the grocery list or whether the car needs servicing — actually I meant to call about that last week — and then it sort of comes back into focus again.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s just as strong the whole time or if I’m just not noticing it for a bit. It doesn’t feel like I get a clean break from it, but I’m not sure how to rate it when I’m distracted.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. It feels like too much, but I don’t know compared to what.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you lost interest in almost everything for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past month? I’m not sure. I mean… the loss of interest thing is definitely happening now. I used to look forward to working on the model railway, and Cubs games with my daughter were sort of our thing. Now the trains are just sitting there half-built on the table, and even turning on a game feels… pointless.\n\nBut if you mean before all this, like earlier in my life, I don’t know that I can point to a time where it was almost everything for two weeks. I’ve had hobbies come and go — I once bought a whole set of woodworking clamps because I thought I’d build a bookshelf, and I don’t think I ever even cut the wood — but that’s probably not the same thing. So… I’m not sure about before this past month. Right now, yes, but before, I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been sleeping differently than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I’m not sleeping like I used to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and just lie there for hours, because the house is so quiet now. It’s funny, I used to complain about noise—TV on, doors closing, her walking around upstairs—and now the quiet feels almost… loud, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, I’m up again around three or four in the morning. I just stare at the ceiling, and my mind starts going through everything I’ve messed up, one thing after another. Once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this past month, was there ever a time when you had trouble sleeping like this for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"I lie in bed for hours with the house too quiet, then wake up around three or four in the morning and can't get back to sleep because my mind starts running through everything I've messed up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before this past month… I don’t think so, not like this. I mean, I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well—everybody does, right? Like if there was some bill I forgot to pay, or when the furnace was making that clicking noise and I kept thinking it was going to quit in the middle of winter.

But this is different. This past month I’ll lie in bed for hours because the house is just too quiet, and then I’ll wake up at three or four in the morning and my head starts going through everything I’ve messed up, and I can’t get back to sleep.

Before now, I don’t remember it going on like that for two weeks or anything.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F43.2, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I just can’t get away from this feeling that I’ve failed. As a father, mainly, and as a useful person. It keeps going around in my head, over and over.\n\nI used to think if you worked hard enough, you know, you’d have something to point to, something that proved you mattered. My dad used to say that—well, not exactly that, but he was always fixing things, couldn’t sit still. Anyway… I don’t feel like that. I feel like I don’t deserve much of a future, if I’m honest.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. Not like—well, I don’t know how to say it without making it sound dramatic. I’ve had these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then the other day I caught myself going through my finances, almost like I was trying to get things in order for someone else, and that… that scared me. I used to be very organized with money anyway, my father was like that too, kept every receipt in little folders, but this felt different. Like I wasn’t just budgeting. It really frightened me.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It really got bad about a month ago, when my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI mean, I knew it was coming, obviously. We bought the dorm stuff and argued about whether she needed this ridiculous little lamp—she did, apparently—and I drove around for weeks acting like it was all normal. But after she left, the house just felt… different. Empty in a way I wasn’t ready for. That’s when this all seemed to start.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “most days” is hard for me to measure. Since she left, things have felt off, definitely. The house is quiet in this strange way—like even the refrigerator sounds louder, which sounds ridiculous.\n\nI keep finding myself noticing little things she left behind, like a hair tie or one of those water bottles she never washed properly, and then I end up thinking about whether I was enough for her, whether I did it right. So… yeah, I’ve felt low, I guess. But I don’t know how to count the days exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I think she’s kind of the backdrop to everything right now, so it’s hard to separate it out.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally ordinary—standing in the kitchen, looking at the mail, and then I can’t tell if I’m sad because of her leaving or just because everything feels… heavy. And then I get distracted thinking about how I used to sort the mail with my wife years ago, we had this little basket that was always overflowing, and I hated that thing… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t really know how to tell when I’m not thinking about her, because it all sort of blends together.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you feel depressed before you start thinking about your daughter leaving?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Mornings are… fuzzy. I wake up and there’s this moment where I’m just aware the room is quiet, and then my brain starts filling in the reasons, you know?\n\nSometimes I’m thinking about her right away, sometimes I’m thinking about whether I left the coffee filter in from yesterday, or how the light comes through the blinds differently this time of year. My wife used to hate those blinds, actually—we talked about replacing them for years and never did.\n\nSo… I don’t know if the depressed feeling comes first or the thoughts about my daughter come first. It’s hard for me to separate them that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been able to keep up with your usual daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still do little things here and there, but the normal stuff is… stacking up.\n\nBills are sitting in piles, and I keep telling myself I’ll deal with them after coffee, or after lunch, or tomorrow. There are house projects I started and then just sort of abandoned. I used to be pretty good about keeping track of all that, actually—had folders and lists and everything. My daughter used to tease me about the labels on the folders.\n\nAnd I’m not really seeing people either. There were a few people from book club, and I used to go on rides sometimes, but I’ve just… stopped. I don’t answer messages the way I should.\n\nI guess the part that makes me uncomfortable saying it out loud is that I started organizing my finances like someone else might need to understand them. So no, I don’t think I’m keeping up, and I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Even when you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel like you’ve failed and don’t deserve much of a future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, my daughter leaving is the thing my mind grabs onto, so it’s hard to pretend that isn’t part of it, but even when I’m not actively thinking about her, there’s this… verdict in my head, I guess.\n\nLike I failed as a father. And not just that—I failed at being someone useful, someone who has a reason to be here. I came in because I can’t shake that. It loops and loops until it feels like I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about all the practical things, like the boxes in the garage I never sorted, or old tax folders—sorry, I know that’s off track. But yes. The feeling is there even when I’m not specifically thinking about her.\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel enjoyable anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts of it.\n\nI used to really look forward to working on the model railway. I had these little sections laid out on the table, tiny buildings, track pieces, all of that. There’s one part where I was trying to make the tunnel look realistic, and I kept buying the wrong kind of glue for it—there are more kinds of glue than anyone needs, honestly. But now it just sits there half-built. I walk past it and think I should do something, and then… nothing.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch them together, or at least argue about them together. Now even turning a game on feels pointless. Like I’ll pick up the remote and then set it back down because I can’t imagine caring what happens.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been present nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day. It’s been about a month now, I think.\n\nIt started after my daughter left for college out of state. I remember the day pretty clearly because I kept fussing with the boxes in the car, like that was going to make any difference, and then afterward the house just felt… too quiet. Since then it hasn’t really let up. So yes, definitely more than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone does. There were times I was down for a while, especially around work changes and family things, but I don’t know if I’d call it like this.\n\nTwo weeks or longer… I’m trying to think. It’s funny, I can remember where I put some ridiculous receipt from years ago, but not how long I felt bad in, say, 2016. I’d have to really think about it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind clearly the way this past month has.\n\nDoctor: During the past month, have you felt depressed for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to measure “most of the day,” exactly. It’s there a lot, that heavy feeling, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like realizing the porch light has been out for weeks or trying to remember whether I paid the water bill, and I’ll think, does that count as not feeling depressed for a minute?\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer. A lot of days have felt pretty low, but “most of the day nearly every day” — I’m not completely sure how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem much stronger than you would normally expect after a child leaves for college?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, people say it’s normal to feel sad when your kid leaves, right? My sister kept saying, “Empty nest, it’ll pass,” and then she started talking about repainting her son’s old room, which… I don’t know, I can’t even imagine doing that.\n\nBut stronger than normal? I don’t know what normal is supposed to feel like. I know it hit me hard, harder than I expected, but whether that’s “too much” or just me not handling it well… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: When you’re occupied with something else and not thinking about your daughter leaving, does the depression still feel just as strong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. Like, if I’m doing something else, sometimes I’ll realize I’ve gone ten minutes thinking about the grocery list or whether the car needs servicing — actually I meant to call about that last week — and then it sort of comes back into focus again.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s just as strong the whole time or if I’m just not noticing it for a bit. It doesn’t feel like I get a clean break from it, but I’m not sure how to rate it when I’m distracted.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, sure. Everybody has, I guess. There were times when I felt pretty down, like when things were changing or I wasn’t sure what I was doing with myself, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks or longer… I honestly don’t know how to pin that down.\n\nI’m bad with timelines. I’ll think something lasted a week and then realize it was a month, or the other way around. Like, I still keep old calendars in a drawer for no reason, and sometimes I look at them and think, what was I even doing that year?\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say clearly, no. I don’t have a specific time I can point to and say, yes, that was two weeks straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your reaction has been out of proportion to your daughter leaving for college?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Out of proportion” is hard for me to judge, because she’s my daughter, you know? It feels enormous to me, but then I start thinking maybe any parent would feel that way if the house suddenly got quiet.\n\nAnd then I think about how other people handle it—my neighbor was joking about turning his son’s room into an office, and I remember thinking, how are you joking about that? But he also has this whole workshop in his garage, he’s always building birdhouses or shelves or whatever, so maybe he just has more to keep him busy.\n\nSo… I can’t really say if it’s out of proportion. It feels like too much, but I don’t know compared to what.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you lost interest in almost everything for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past month? I’m not sure. I mean… the loss of interest thing is definitely happening now. I used to look forward to working on the model railway, and Cubs games with my daughter were sort of our thing. Now the trains are just sitting there half-built on the table, and even turning on a game feels… pointless.\n\nBut if you mean before all this, like earlier in my life, I don’t know that I can point to a time where it was almost everything for two weeks. I’ve had hobbies come and go — I once bought a whole set of woodworking clamps because I thought I’d build a bookshelf, and I don’t think I ever even cut the wood — but that’s probably not the same thing. So… I’m not sure about before this past month. Right now, yes, but before, I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been sleeping differently than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I’m not sleeping like I used to.\n\nI’ll get into bed and just lie there for hours, because the house is so quiet now. It’s funny, I used to complain about noise—TV on, doors closing, her walking around upstairs—and now the quiet feels almost… loud, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, I’m up again around three or four in the morning. I just stare at the ceiling, and my mind starts going through everything I’ve messed up, one thing after another. Once that starts, I can’t really get back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time when you had trouble sleeping like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Before this past month… I don’t think so, not like this. I mean, I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well—everybody does, right? Like if there was some bill I forgot to pay, or when the furnace was making that clicking noise and I kept thinking it was going to quit in the middle of winter.\n\nBut this is different. This past month I’ll lie in bed for hours because the house is just too quiet, and then I’ll wake up at three or four in the morning and my head starts going through everything I’ve messed up, and I can’t get back to sleep.\n\nBefore now, I don’t remember it going on like that for two weeks or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’ll think something lasted a week and then realize it was a month, or the other way around. Before this past month… I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches, sure. Everybody does, I guess. There were times I felt down, or discouraged, especially when things weren’t going the way I thought they would.
